233. DENNIS SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of Nov., 1 bag, value 8 d.; and 5lbs. weight of rags; the goods of John Lewis.
JOHN FLOWS . I am a marine-store dealer, in High-street, Shad well. The prisoner had been to my shop to sell things—a little after six o'clock, on the 29th of'Nov., some one gave me information, and I missed a Guernsey bag, made of sugar bags, containing about 20lbs. of canvass rags—it was safe about three in the afternoon—the prisoner had been in my shop about eleven—he came again about seven for a pair of shoes he had left in the morning—I gave him in charge—this is the bag; and the rags.
HENRY HOLDFORTH . I live in Glazier's-court, White cross-street. I was sent in search of the prisoner—I went to Mr. Baldry's shop, in Fox-lane, and while I was there the prisoner came in—he had this bag on his head—I said, "This is the very thing I am looking for"—he said it was his own, and tried to get away—I took hold of him, a mob came round, and I was obliged to let him go.
WILLIAM BLOAG . I am a policeman. A little before eight o'clock that evening the prisoner was given into my custody by Mr. Lewis—he said he never stole the bag, nor the rags, but some boy gave him a halfpenny to sell them.
Prisoner's Defence. A young chap came up; he said, "Carry this for me, and I will give you a halfpenny."
CORNELIUS SULLIVAN . I am a mariner, and live in Shad well-market. The prisoner is my brother—I have to look after him the best way I can—last Tuesday evening, between five and six o'clock, when I had done work, I came in, and he was sitting by the fire with no boots on—I was asking him what had become of his boots—he said he was short in the morning, he had gone to this marine-store dealer's and pledged them for 2 d.—I went with him to look after them—the prosecutor said they were backwards, and he took the boy and locked him in the kitchen, sent for a policeman, and during that time the witness came in with the bag, and they gave him in charge—I do not know how he came to want 2 d. there was breakfast in the house for him to have.
JOHN LEWIS re-examined. I lent the prisoner 2 d. to get some breakfast.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 14.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Ten Days, and Whipped.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18421128-234]
234. THOMAS DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of Sept., 1 looking-glass and frame, value 5 l., the goods of George Rogers and another; and EDWARD WADDILOVE , for inciting, moving, procuring, counselling, and commanding the said Thomas Davis to commit the said felony.—3rd COUNT, for harbouring and maintaining the said Thomas Davis.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BODKIN conducted the Prosecution,
JOSEPH KIBBLEWHITE . I live in New Church-street, Padding ton. In Sept. last I was employed as agent to let a house at No. 45, Bedford-place, near Bayswater—Davis saw me on the subject in the early part of Sept.—I let him the house by the year—the rent was 36 l. a year, to commence on the 29th of Sept.—I had a man near the spot, who had the key, and who gave it to him—he described himself as an artist, and referred me to Mr. Hubbert, No. 12, George-yard—I went there, and saw a person who called himself Hubbert.
WILLIAM DEAR . I am an upholsterer, and carry on business with George Rogers, in St. George's-place, Hyde-park. On the 19th of Sept. Davis ordered some goods to be sent to No. 45, Bedford-place—he gave me his card, and said he had taken the house on a five years' lease; his wife was in York-shire; but in the course of a week he would bring her to select some more goods—in consequence of what he said, I sent some carpets into the house on the 22nd, and on the 28th I sent a glass there by Triphook, in consequence of a message brought to me by Sharman—it was a stock glass, used as a patten—it was only to show the style of it—there was no price or bill of parcels sent—I saw it next on the 5th of Oct., at Mr. King's, in Holborn—I got the duplicate from a woman, who went by the name of Waddilove, at No. 1, Chapel-place, Borough—it was a very small house, one story above and one below, a very different house to that in Bedford-place—I went there on Tuesday, the 5th of Oct.—this is my glass.
JOHN SHARMAN . I am in the service of Rogers and Dear. On the 27th of Sept. I received directions from my master to go to No. 45, Bedford-place—it was a respectable-looking house—I saw a servant, called William—after that I was putting down some carpets, and Davis desired me to measure the chimney-piece, to have a glass put in hand for it—I measured it, and brought the order home for it—my master had not a glass of the size ordered—I went next day to complete the laying down of the carpets—I saw Davis there, Waddilove, William, and a female called Mary—William invited me down stairs to take something—when I got to the kitchen I found Mary and Waddi-love—Waddilove said he had come to invite Mary, his sister-in-law, to a christening—Mary said she could not go, she expected her young mistress home from Yorkshire, but they could come to her on Sunday, and if her mistress had not come home, they could come into the house, and spend the evening there—William said his master had just married a young lady, worth 5000 l.—I cannot say whether Waddilove heard it, but he was there—he was tipsy at the time—William said there would be furniture wanted for the drawing-room—in consequence of what passed, I desired to know from Davis, through William, whether it would be desirable to look at a pattern glass—he appeared to go up, and come down—he said Mr. Davis would wait at home till half-past nine o'clock to-morrow morning—Davis then had a fine head of black hair—whether it was a wig or not I cannot say—it appeared to be kept in very good order.
RICHARD WILLIAM TRIPHOOK . I am in the employ of Rogers and Dear. On the morning of the 28th of Sept. I was sent with this chimney-glass to No. 45, Bedford-place—I asked for Mr. Davis—I saw William—they said Davis was at home—I took the glass in, and asked for Mr. Davis—William said he was not at home—I left the glass for him to see—I said I would send up the next day—I was asked to take a glass of ale, but declined it.
JOHN CLARIDGE . I am in the service of Mr. King, a pawnbroker, in High-Holborn. On the 30th of Sept. I took this glass in pledge, for three guineas, in the name of "John Jones, housekeeper, 10, Shoe-lane"—I believe Davis is the man who brought it, but he is so much altered in appearance, I should not like to swear to him—he had black hair at that time—this is the duplicate I gave. GEORGE ROGERS. I am in partnership with Mr. Dear—we are upholsterers. We received an order from Davis—on the 30th of Sept. I went to No. 45, Bedford-place—the house was shut—I could not get in—there was a bill up to let it, and it appeared to me to be empty—on the 4th of Oct. I went with a policeman to a small house in Chapel-place, Borough, with two rooms, nothing like the house in Bedford-place—I knocked at the door, and Waddilove appeared—I said, "I believe Mr. Davis is here; I want to see him on a charge of taking a looking-glass of mine"—Waddilove said, "He is not here, and you shan't come in"—I put one foot in the door to prevent its being shut—he tried to shut me in between the door and door-post, but, with the assistance of a policeman, I forced myself in—I said I would search the house for Davis, as I believed him to be there—Waddilove said, "By God, nobody shall go up stairs"—we had a very severe scuffle together—he tried to get me by* the throat—I forced his hand from there—he seized my coat, tore it, struck me on my breast, and broke my watch glass—I forced my way from him, got up stairs, and saw Davis—I called to the officer that Davit was there, and begged him to come and take him—Waddilove had the tongs in his hand, trying to strike the policemen with them, and they were holding him at the time—he said, "Davis is my master, I will protect him to the last moment of my existence"—he said he had never been to No. 45, Bedford-place, Bays water, and did not know where it was—I saw Mrs. Waddilove there—I saw Davis pass something small over to her.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You sent some carpets on the 27th? A. Yes—I never got them again, nor the money for them—I said, when I took him, it was for swindling us of carpets and a glass.
JAMES HARRISON ( police-constable M 195.) I accompanied Mr. Rogers to Chapel-place, which is a narrow turning out of Suffolk-street—Mr. Rogers knocked at the door—Waddilove opened it—he said Davis was not there, neither should we come in there—a scuffle then took place between Rogers and Waddilove—Rogers was thrown down on his back in the room—he got away, and got up stairs.
FREDERICK LITCHFIELD ( police-constable M 50.) I went to this honse—Waddilove said to Davis, "Don't go, don't go—you are my master, I will protect you."
DAVIS — GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Seven Years.
WADDILOVE — GUILTY. Aged 33,— GUILTY on the 3 rd Count — Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18421128-235]
235. WILLIAM JOHNSON SAWYER was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Oct., 16 yards of ribbon, value 13 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Gouger. MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution,
JOHN GOUGER . I am a warehouseman, and live in Wood-street—I have frequently dealt with the prisoner for artificial flowers—he and others came every
morning to sell their flowers—sometimes I have had twenty or fifty in a morning—I select what I want, and pay for them on Saturday—on the 24th of Oct, there were some pieces of ribbon which had been remitted from the country—this is one—( looking at it)—it has not been blocked—it was lying on my counter, for the inspection of my customers—it is 9 d. a-yard—the pattern is now inside—before they are sold it is the practice to block them, and place the pattern outside—the prisoner would have to pass the counter where these ribbons were—none of these ribbons had been sold from my premises unblocked—they were manufactured for me by my nephew—I gave the prisoner in custody on the 23rd of Nov.—he had been on my premises between the 23rd of Oct. and the 11th of Nov.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Can you tell the number of yards in it, from looking at it? A. No—I have missed ribbons, but not this identical piece—this is my nephew's own pattern, made exclusively for me.
WILLIAM ACKLAND . I live in Phoenix-street, Somers-town—my wife keeps a haberdasher's shop—up to the 23rd of Nov. I had been living as assistant to Mr. Fox well, a haberdasher, in Judd-street—the prisoner used to come there to sell artificial flowers—about a month before he was taken he offered me this piece of ribbon for sale—I purchased it of him—he told me he took it in exchange for flowers of his brother, who lived in Chiswell-street—I said I did not want it—he said he would sell it to me cheap—I asked the price of it—he said 6 1/2 d. or 7 d.—I cannot say which—I ultimately bought it at 5 d. a-yard—there were sixteen yards, for which I paid him 6 s. 8 d.—it was the same as it is now, unblocked, and the wrong side outwards—I afterwards offered the same ribbon for sale to Mr. Lawrence.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean you were unable to judge of the value? A. No—I have bought ribbons at warehouses for 4 d. a-yard, and they were worth 1s—I did not entertain any suspicion—I have been dismissed from my situation in consequence of this.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you ever raise a word about the prisoner getting it from his brother-in-law till this time, or is it since you have been discharged that you have thought of that? A. No—I gave the same deposition before the Magistrate—when we buy ribbons at 4 d. which cost 9 d., it is what we call job goods—this is not a new style of ribbon.
JOHN LAWRENCE . I live in Chapel-street, Somers-town. On Wednesday, the 16th of Nov., Ackland came to me and brought this ribbon—he left it for 9 s. 6 d.—I went to Mr. Gouger's warehouse, and saw the same pattern—I said I had got half a piece, and gave it up.
JAMES ALLEN ( police-constable E 159.) I took the prisoner on the 23rd of Nov.—I was present at his examination before Mr. Greenwood, on this charge—I heard him asked what he had to say, and saw the clerk write it down, and the Magistrate sign it—( read)—"The prisoner says," I have nothing to say but what I said before, which I wish to say again; I received the ribbon from a person named Thompson, at a public-house in Westmore-land-buildings, in exchange for flowers, two dozen and four bunches; be gave me the ribbons and 10 s.; there were several flower-makers whom I knew there in company; it was this day month, four weeks to-day; Thompson said he wanted my goods to go into the country; I cannot say whether Miles was present; he has been there generally these last two months on a Saturday; I did not tell Ackland I bought the ribbon of my brother; I said be was in the ribbon trade, and he told me the value of the ribbon; I expect, on the trial, to get parties who saw me take the ribbon in exchange for flowers. The prisoner called
MILES. I am an artificial flower-maker—I know the prisoner—
I was not present at any place where he purchased ribbon of this kind—I have been in this public-house in Westmoreland-buildings, Alders gate-street—I was not there when the prisoner bought any goods of Thompson.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-236]
236. CHARLES LEE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM LEECH . I am cashier to Alexander Hatfield and Co., who carry on the business of Taddy and others, in the Minories—a person who I believe to be the prisoner called on me, and produced this card—( looking at it)—from receiving it, I believed he was carrying on business under the firm of Lee and Gold, in Cooper'8-row, it being in the neighbourhood of many respectable wine merchants—he ordered some cheroots and cigars about the 5th of Oct. he was supplied—the prisoner afterwards came and paid for them—on the 15th of Oct., be called as the representative still of Lee and Gold—he said he had just received an order for wine for the country by the post, and wanted 20lbs. weight of cheroots, and 7lbs. of tobacco—I said, "7lbs. is a quantity we cannot send out; I suppose you want it for a sample"—he said, "Yes, I do"—we were busy that evening, and did not send them—about eleven o'clock the next day the prisoner came again, and said, "You did not send those goods I ordered, but I made a mistake; it is not 7lbs., it is 14lbs. of tobacco; I will take it with me, the cheroots will do in two hours"—one of the men gave him the tobacco in my presence, believing it was for Lee and Gold, wine-merchants, Cooper row, Tower-hill—the 20lbs. of cheroots were sent, by my direction, about two hours after, with a written request that they should be paid for, having no account—in consequence of something I heard afterwards, I went to the place about twenty minutes past one o'clock—I found the 20lbs. of cheroots in the hands of a broker—the prisoner had come, on sundry occasions, as from Lee and Gold—on the 15th he came, to my certain knowdge—he said he wanted 20lbs. of cheroots for Lee and Gold—he did not say" wine-merchants"—he said the goods were to be sent to No. 14, Cooper's-row, the same as the sample—I paid the broker 5 l. 4 s. for the cheroots—I believed that Lee and Gold lived in Cooper's-row—I only knew they lived there merely from the representation they had given with the card—I connected the prisoner with the card by his saying he wanted those cheroots and tobacco for Lee and Gold, to inclose with wine to be sent that night,
JOHN HOLLAND . I am a porter, in the service of Messrs. Hatfield and Co.—I was desired to take 20lbs. of cheroots to Lee and Gold, No. 14, Cooper's row, wine-merchants—I took them there—when I got there the counting-house was locked, and a paper in it, "Return on half on hour"—I did not find the prisoner there—I waited some time, and then a young man came—he said be bad just been and had some dinner—I showed him the goods—he said, You had no occasion to wait, you might have left them in the house"—I said, "I did not know that"—he went into the counting-house—I left the invoice—he read it, and said, "It is all right"—I said yet, but I wanted the money—he said, "Mr. Lee is out, but if you call at four o'clock he will be in, and very likely pay you"—I went again at a quarter past four, and found a broker in the house, and the cheroots in his possession.
WILLIAM CHILDS . I am a City police-constable. I attended with a broker, to distrain at No. 14, Cooper's row—there was no wine in the cellars, only two empty bottles, but I found a parcel of letters from where they had been writing, to all parts of the country—I found a packet of cheroots, which the broker immediately pounced upon—the prisoner came in about four o'clock—I showed him the packets of cheroots, and said. "What about these cheroots?"
—he said, "I know nothing at all about them"—he did not tell me he had ordered them of Taddy—I had seen him go to Mr. Taddy's in the morning, and bring the tobacco away—I said, "You must know something about these cheroots"—he said, "I know nothing about the cigars"—I said to the man, "Go and fetch Mr. Taddy down"—the prisoner said, "For God's sake, let me go"—the other person was present—he said, "I took the parcel, but Mr. Lee is there"—he was in the counting-house at the time—he had been there all day, and had heard the prisoner say that.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not offer Mr. Taddy's man to take them back? A. No—they were condemned.
JONATHAN BARBER . I am in the Excise. There is such a place as Cooper's-row—there are some wine-merchants there, licensed to carry on business—there are no such persons as Lee and Gold licensed to carry on business there.
Prisoner's Defence. All the goods I have had I have paid for generally, and have lost nearly 3000 l. in business; I never was a bankrupt. I have always paid every body.
GUILTY of Conspiracy. Aged 39.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18421128-237]
237. THOMAS HAMER MORRISS and JOHN HORNER were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
FANNY CRAWFORD . I am in the employ of Victor Dumar, an importer and dealer in French shoes, in Regent-street. On the 23rd of Sept. a person named William Clark called, and asked me to show him some French goods—I asked if it was for wholesale or retail, he said wholesale that he was living at Epsom, and had a large shop there—he had dealt in English shoes, but his customers had wanted French goods, and he came in, seeing Mr. Dumar's name on the door—he said he should require 30 l. or 40 l. worth, as a sample of each sort—he said, "What are Mr. Dumar's terms?"—I said, "Ready money, with two and a half discount, or three months' credit; we must have a reference, if we give credit"—he said, "I can give you a very good reference, one that I am sure you will be satisfied with"—he then handed me a bill, with "Thomas Hamer, 20, Bread-street-hill" on it—he requested that Mr. Durnar would go on the following morning, before eleven o'clock, for the reference, as Mr. Hamer was then most likely to be in the way—on the next day I went to No. 20, Bread-street-hill—I asked for Mr. Hamer, and the prisoner Morris said, "I am Mr. Hamer"—I saw the name of Thomas Hamer on the side of the door—I said Mr. William Clark, of Epsom, had referred Mr. Dumar to him, as to his capability of paying for goods—he said, "Yes, I know Mr. Clark very well indeed"—he said that he had served him with goods ever since he had opened his shop, and had always found him very punctual in his payments, his bills were always taken np before they were due, at the end of six weeks or two months—he said, "What kind of goods are they?"—I said, "French boots and shoes"—he said, "Of course they are of the first quality; because I know that Clark's customers are all gentlefolks; there are a great many gentlemen's seats about Epsom, which of course you are aware of"—I asked if we could give credit with propriety—he said, "Certainly, without the slightest danger"—I said, "Has he been established long"—he said, "Somewhere about three years"—there was a counting-house and a warehouse—I did not look further—I thought, from the appearance, he was a very extensive merchant—when I went back, on the same day, Clark called again—he said, "Have you called on Mr. Hamer this morning?" I said, "Yes, I called on him, and Mr. Dunn was perfectly satisfied with the character he has given you"—before he went away he had eighteen pairs
of shoes—he said he was in want of them, as he had received an order, and on the Monday or Tuesday he called with a horse and cart, and took away the remainder, amounting to 37 l. 11 s. 6 d.—I cautioned him about the danger of French goods being exposed to the weather—he said "Oh, I have provided against that, I have a very large glass case"—I said if not, they would be soiled—he promised to call in a week and pay 10 l.—I have never seen the 10 l.—I was present when he gave a bill for 27 l. 11 s. 6 d.—I saw him write the bill—I believe. these papers to be his handwriting.
VICTOR DUMAR . Crawford was in my service—I have never received payment for these boots and shoes from Mr. Clark or Hamer—I went down to Epsom—I found no shop with a handsome glass case.
JOSEPH ELSWORTH PETCH . About the 21st of Oct. I saw a person who said his name was William Clark—he said he was residing at Epsom, and he wanted some boots—he referred me to Mr. Hamer, of Bread-street-hill—I went there, and saw Morris—he said, "My name is Hamer"—Clark had said he was about to open a shop, and Morris said he was about to supply him with 60 l. worth of goods; he knew his family, and knew him to be a respectable man—I asked as to his honesty and Industry—he gave him a very excellent character—Clark obtained from me goods to the amount of 24 l. odd—he paid me 5 l —the term I bad proposed was half cash—he took away my goods in a cart on the 28th of Oct.—I have never seen him since—if the reference had not been satisfactory I certainly should not have parted with my goods—the premises of Hamer were well stocked with crates and other merchandise.
THOMAS FOOT PIPER . I am a stay-maker. On the 27th of Oct. William Clark came to me—he said be lived at Epsom, and wanted some goods—I required a reference—he gave me one to Thomas Hamer, Bread-street-hill—I sent my clerk there, and from his representation I ultimately parted with some goods—I have not seen Clark since.
JOHN KNIGHT . I am clerk to Mr. Piper. I went by his direction to No. 20, Bread-street-hill—I inquired for Mr. Hamer—Morris came forward and said his name was Hamer—I told him I came to inquire the character of William Clark, of Epsom—he said, "I know Mr. Clark very well, walk into my counting-house"—he gave him an excellent character, said he knew his family, and he had no doubt we should be very safe in giving him credit—he said he thought we were perfectly safe in trusting him—at that time there were goods strewed about the warehouse in large quantities—I thought it had the appearance of a place of large business.
RICHARD SWIFT . I am a boot-maker, and live in Greville-street, Hattongarden. On the 1st of November Clark called on me, and said his name was William Clark, of High-street, Epsom, and he wanted a quantity of boots and shbes, about 40 l. worth—he referred me to Mr. Hamer, of Bread-street-hill—I sent my clerk there, and after a report from him I delivered to Clark goods to the amount of 39 l. 14 s. with a bill of the goods, charging the hamper 12 s., if not returned—Clark came with a horse and cart, and took them away—I went afterwards with an officer to Hamer's place—we went right through the house—we found several crates stuffed with straw, which were tied down, having the appearance of being full—some of the crates in the bottom warehouse had a few dishes and plates strewed at the top of them, but nothing under them but straw—we found several books, but no entry, except an old one of Jones Lloyd and Co., in 1821.
Cross-examined by MR. WILD. Q. I believe you received some money on account? A. Yes, 15 l.—we found a desk at Hamer's—I think it was shut when we went in, but we opened it—it was not locked. WILLIAM SADD . I am clerk to Mr. Swift. I went to Bread-street-hill, and
saw Morris—he said his name was Hamer—I told him what I came about—he said he had known Mr. Clark some time, that he was a highly respectable man, and we might trust him to twice the amount of goods he had asked us for.
JOHN HOLLAND . I am an occasional customer of Mr. Swift. I know his boots—some of them were exhibited to me for sale, on the 8th of Nov., by a person named Davis—I let it out that I knew Mr. Swift, and I made an appointment for the purchase of the goods—I went to the house where Davis had shown me the goods—he was gone, and they knew nothing about it—I had actually seen some of these same goods in Swift's warehouse—I communicated this to Mr. Swift.
HENRY MATSON . Mr. Dumar lodges with me, I was going into the City, and proffered my assistance—I went, at his request, to No. 20, Bread-street-hill, and took this bill with me—( looking at a bill)—I saw Homer—I said I was about to take the bill; I wished to know if Clark was a responsible man—he said he was in business in Epsom, he knew him to be a very respectable man, and I need have no fear—he said Hamer was not at home, but he would be in shortly.
RICHARD SWIFT re-examined. I went subsequently to the warehouse in Bread-street-hill, and saw Homer—he said he was clerk to Mr. Hamor—I asked him Mr. Hamer's private address—he said he did not know it, he had only been with him seven weeks—he was in a situation in which he might have become acquainted with the crates that were there.
DAVID HEWITT ( City police-constable, No. 233.) I took Horner at a coffee-8hop, in Peter's-lane, St. John-street—a person I went with went in, and asked for Mr. Hamer, and the landlord put his finger out to call, and Horner came out—the person with me said, This is the gentleman," and I asked him to walk with me, and took him to the station.
JOHN HOLLAND re-examined, I was written to by Mr. Hamer, requesting I would send some goods from my warehouse in Queen-street—I went there, and saw Horner—I asked for Mr. Hamer, and Homer introduced Morris to me as Mr. Hamer—I said our terms were ready money—Morris said that would not suit him as his capital was all locked up, and he could not pay till after Christmas—I said that would not do, we could not execute the order. MORRIS — GUILTY.[ Judgement Respited: See original trial image.] Aged 56.— HORNER — GUILTY . Aged 54.— Judgment Respited,

239. SELINA WELLESLEY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JAMES HUGHES . I keep a shop in Red Lion-street, Holborn. I am in the habit of supplying goods to persons who produce a card from Ripsher and Co., haberdashers—on the 17th of June the prisoner brought this card, and threw it down on my table—I delivered to her a pair of boots, a pair of shoes, and a pair of clogs.—( The card was here put in and read as follows.) S. Rips her and Co., linen and woollen-drapers, hatters, hosiers, &c, No. 18, Lisle-street, Leicester-square."— On the back of it.—" 10, Red Lion-street, Holborn, June 2nd, '42.—Mr. Hughes, Let the bearer, Mrs. Singleton, have shoes. H. 405, for S. Rips her.—H. S."
HENRY RIPSHER . I am in the habit of dealing with Mr. Hughes—this is my brother's card, and the writing on it is the writing of the young man in his shop, but it has the name of Singleton on it, not the prisoner's.
JOSEPH SINGLETON . I had this card—the prisoner was living with me, but she absconded on the 17th of June, and took this card out of my drawer, and got the things—I never authorized her to get them.
Prisoner. This is a man of bad character—I knew him a length of time before I went to his house—he wanted to live with me—he lived on my prostitution. Witness. I am a married man—my wife took her in when she was quite destitute, and she robbed us shamefully—she took this card out of my drawer, and took a table-cloth at the same time—I had a child lost the same day that she absconded, on the 17th of June, and here is an advertisement for my child—( producing a placard)—my child was sent home in a cab six days after, very nearly naked.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years,

240. JOHN WAKELING was indicted for embezzlement.
EMMA SMITH . I am a widow, and live in Stratford-grove, Essex. My husband died four months ago—the prisoner was in his employment, and continued in mine afterwards—he received money on my account—at my husband's death, I sent him to a Mr. Flax ton, who was indebted to my husband—he said he had seen Mr. Flaxton, and that he would call on me in a few days—the amount of his bill was 1 l. 3 s. 4 d.—I never received any part of it—the prisoner absconded as soon as it was found out—I had another customer named Coates, part of his bill was due before my husband's death—I gave the prisoner in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q, He came to yon again afterwards? A, Yes, and said he was very sorry for it—I am left sole executrix of my husband's affairs—I carry on the business of a furrier—part of Mr. Coatts's debt, to the amount of 1 l., occurred since my. husband's death—the prisoner worked for me twelve months at 22 s. a week.
HENRY COATES . I had an account with Mrs. Smith, after her husband's death—I paid the prisoner money on her account several times—a good deal more than 1 l., but only 12. on the account since her husband's death—it was on the 6th of Sept.—I have known him to conduct himself very properly.
GEORGE SMITH . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—I told him I supposed he knew what it was for—he said he did perfectly well, and he was sorry of it.
(The prisoner received a good character.) GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.

241. JAMES WOLNER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Nov., 1 live tame duck, price 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Norris.
THOMAS NORRIS . I live at Wood ford. I saw my ducks safe in my yard, on the 14th of Nov., and on the 15th I missed one—I found it wrapped up in a coat on Gibson's hay cart—Gibson's son was driving the cart—the prisoner was with him—the coat it was in belonged to young Gibson.
JAMES WHITE ( police-sergeant, K 13.) I was sent for—the prosecutor said he had missed a duck, and he suspected it was on the cart—I saw him pull the coat out of the cart, and the duck dropped out of it—the prosecutor said he had seen the prisoner wrap it up in the coat, and put it on the cart—Gibson saying the same, I took the prisoner.
WILLIAM BEAVER ( police-constable K 389.) I took charge of the prisoner—I found some wet dirt in his pockets, and some feathers which corresponded with the duck's feathers.
Prisoner. I never touched the duck—it was not in my possession—I had
nothing to do with the cart—I did not know the duck was there—the feathers I had were pigeon's feathers not duck's.
THOMAS NORRIS re-examined. I saw the prisoner put the coat up in the cart. GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined One Month.

247. WILLIAM HENRY FORD was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of Nov., 1 pewter measure, value 1 s., the goods of William Daniels.

Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You told the Magistrate you did not believe he had any intention of stealing it? A. Yes, and the Magistrate ordered him to be discharged on bail—he said that he stooped down outside
the door, saw the measure, and picked it up; but having been drinking, he dropped it again; but he did not do so.
DANIEL REARDON ( police-sergeant R 23.) I saw the prisoner come out of the house, and as he crossed the road I heard the sound of a pewter measure fall—I saw this measure between his legs, and took it up.

248. JAMES OSBORNE and JAMES KELLY were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Nov., 1lb. weight of bacon, value 6 d., the goods of Thomas Atkinson.
ELIZABETH SARAH ATKINSON . I am the wife of Thomas Atkinson, of Greenwich. I saw a piece of bacon safe about half an hour before it was taken—I saw a boy's arm through the broken window, endeavoring to take another piece—I then went to the door, and spoke to the officer—he took the prisoners, but there was no bacon found on them—I missed one piece.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . On the 17th of Nov. I saw Osborne take a piece of bacon, and run up the road with it—Kelly was standing with him, and I had seen them together for a quarter of an hour—Kelly ran up to three other boys, when Osborne had taken it.
Kelly, What he is saying is false; some boys passed us, and we followed them. Witness. I am certain I saw them together—I had watched the five together for a quarter of an hour—I work for my brother-in-law, who it a wood-chopper.
JOHN BRADRICK ( police-constable R 64.) I received information, and took Osborne on the Blackheath-road, running—Kelly was about 100 yards behind him, with three other boys, who were discharged by the Magistrate—Kelly gave his name as Mason.

250. WILLIAM RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of Nov., 2 1/2 yards of woollen-cloth, value 30 s.; 1 jacket, 10 s.; 12 collars, 3 s.; 12 shirts, 24 s.; 1 1/2 yard of satin, 4 s.; 3 yards of calico, 2 s.; and 1 waistcoat, 4 s.; the goods of John Moore, his master.
JOHN MOORE . I am a pawnbroker, and live at Woolwich. The prisoner was my warehouse-boy—Hunter, one of my men, brought me a bundle containing the articles produced, which are mine—I then asked the prisoner to
give me the key of his box—I and my young man examined his box, and in it were various things, and amongst them these twelve shirts, a remnant of cloth, a jacket and waistcoat—he said his mother had bought them in Ragfair—I have not the slightest doubt that they are pledges, but I cannot swear to them—I can swear to these collars, and part of this satin.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you any personal knowledge on the subject? A. Not any—these collars appear to have been dirtied since they have been in my possession—they are quite new—only one has been worn—they do not bear any mark—I have a duplicate relating to these collars, which I found in the prisoner's pocket, for "12 collars, 1 s. 6 d., 19th August, 1841," in the name of "Ann Thompson, Church-street"—this is my ticket of the articles—it is the one I kept—the customer is not here—it is the handwriting of one of my young men.
ROBERT HUNTER . I am in the prosecutor's service. I took in these twelve collars—this duplicate is my writing, and ought to have been on these collars in the shop.
(The prisoner received a good character; and a gentleman engaged to take him as an apprentice.)
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Seven Days.

251. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of Nov., 2 wine-glasses, value 1 s., the goods of William Bunder.

Prisoner, I went in with some poultry in my basket; the glasses were standing before me; my right hand was in my pocket, and the basket on my left arm.

252. MARY TOOMEY and MARY MAHONEY were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Nov., 2 handkerchiefs, value 12 s., the goods of Henry Castle.
CHARLES JAMES PARKER . I am apprentice to Henry Castle, of Blackheath. On the 21st of Nov. the prisoners came to the shop for some black silk handkerchiefs—I showed them what we had got—I reached down some coloured handkerchiefs, and pointed out two—when the prisoners were gone I missed them—I told my master—I went out, and could not see them—I believe these handkerchiefs to be the same I lost—I cannot positively swear to them—I lost them about one o'clock.
JOHN FINCH . I produce these two handkerchiefs—Me prisoners offered them to me to pledge on the 21st of Nov.—I live about three miles and a half from the prosecutor's, on the road to London.
Toomey. Q. Can you swear it was me that pawned them? A. I cannot swear positively, but to the best of my belief they are the two persons.
Mahoney's Defence. I never had either of the handkerchiefs.

253. MARY TOOMEY and MARY MAHONEY were again indicted for stealing, on the 5th of Nov., 2 sheets, value 2 s., the goods of William Hoile.
ELEANOR HOILE . I am the wife of William Hoile. The prisoners took furnished lodgings of me—they left two weeks rent in debt—this sheet is mine—here is my work—it is made into a chemise now.
SARAH HARE . I am searcher at the station—I found this shift on Mahoney—she told me she made it in Ireland.
Toomey's Defence. On Friday morning we went out, and in our absence she took our bed-things out of the press, and took what we bad bought for ourselves; we stopped there till the next morning; I declare the sheets were left in the press when we went out; this is done out of spite.
Mahoney's Defence. It is through spite because I gave some water to a woman in the house; she took our things in our absence; we went in and lay on the boards till the morning, and she said nothing about the sheet.

255. DANIEL PORTER was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN TYLER . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live at Deptford The prisoner was in my employ—it was his duty to receive money from my customers, and bring it to me—last Wednesday I sent him to sweep the chimney at Mrs. Capeskin's—I asked him how many chimneys he had swept—he said, one, and that led into another—if he had swept two he ought to have brought me If.—he only gave me 6 d.
ANN COBETSKI . I live in Elizabeth-terrace, New Cross. On the 30th of Nov. the prisoner came and swept two chimneys for me, and I paid him 1 s., for Mr. Tyler.
JOHN ROSEVEAR ( police-sergeant R 9.) I took the prisoner—I cautioned him—he said he had taken it in consequence of his master giving him such low wages as 1 s. 6 d. a week—he also said it was customary for sweeps to embezzle their masters' money.
JOHN TYLER re-examined. There is no such custom—I give him 1 s. 6 d. a week, with food and lodging.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months,

256. HENRY GATES was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Sept., I shilling, 1 sixpence, 3 pence, and 6 halfpence, the monies of Robert Carey, his master.
SUSANNAH HCNT . I am in the service of Robert Carey, a farmer, who has a wharf at the Surrey Canal-bridge, Old Kent-road, The prisoner was in his service four years up to the 11th of Oct.—I was in the habit of paying him 5 s. 6 d. a week, on Mr. Carey's account, for bran for the horses—on the 24th of Sept. I gave him five shillings and a sixpence for that purpose—I had directions from my master to give him that money—he had 5 s. 6 d. for the bran every week for twenty-nine weeks running—he always told me he fetched it from Walters'—it was left to his discretion where to get it—he always came to me for the money.
HENRY WALTERS . I am a miller, and live at the Surrey Canal-bridge,

[trialAccount t18421128-257]
257. ROSETTA DAUGHTERS was indicted for bigamy. MATILDA DREWITT . I am the wife of Michael George Drewitt, and live at No. 100, Waterloo-road, Lambeth. I first knew the prisoner by the name of Rosetta White—I was present at the parish church at Clapham when she was married to Robert Daughters, my husband's cousin—I had attended the church the Sunday before, and heard them asked—Daughters was a single man, and was a carman, working in Tower-street—he had lodged in the parish of Clapham some time before—I only saw the prisoner about four times before she was married—the first time was about a month before—I think Mr. Murray was the clergyman that married them—I went and got the certificate from the church after they were married—the clerk gave it me—I examined it with the register at the parish church—I saw it written from the book—I saw them once afterwards at their door, in Kent-street, but did not go in—I saw Robert Daughters alive yesterday.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How came you to go to the church to get this? A. Daughters's mother asked me to go with her—she and Daughters prosecute this case—I have known Daughters nine years—I have seen him a great number of times—I cannot say how many—I do not know where he was in 1839—he has been a soldier, and has been at different parts—I believe he has not been to India—I do not know what regiment he was in—he is not in the same regiment now—he first turned soldier five years ago—he was a carman when they were married—I do not know how soon after the marriage he turned soldier—he was constantly absent—he has been to Leeds, and several other places, I believe—his wife did not go with him—I do not know where he was in July, 1841—I did not see him then—I went down to Chatham to tee him last year, in the depot—I cannot say how long he was there—I did not go to see him anywhere else—I cannot say whether he behaved well to his wife—I do not know anything about her buying some furniture, and his selling it, and making use of it—I have heard it was the case, but her mother says it was not—I have heard of his behaving ill to her, from her mother, and then going away as a soldier—he is a soldier now—he went to Chatham yesterday.
MICHAEL GEORGE DREWITT . I am a lamp-lighter—the last witness is my wife. I was present when the prisoner was married to Robert Daughters, on the 16th of February—I knew both the parties—she went by the name of Rosetta White then.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you tell how soon he went for a soldier? A. No, I cannot—I signed my name, "George Drewitt," to the register.
WILLIAM ST. JOHN TURNER . I am parish clerk of the district church of St. Peter's, Walworth. I produce a copy of the register of marriage—I was present at the prisoner's marriage—she gave orders for the banns to be put up—I think I saw her twice or thrice on the subject—I made the extract from the register, and examined it—it is a true copy—( The extract stated that William Highwood, batchelor, and Rachael Smith, spinster, were married on the 11th July, 1841, by George Anslie, minister, in presence of H. Dove and W. S. Turner.)
Cross-examined. Q. Dove I suppose was a friend of the par ties? A.
Yes, I believe he is not here—I do not recollect all the young women that come to be married—the prisoner made her mark—I thought there was something rather particular in her appearance when she came to put up the banns—I have been thirty-eight years in the neighbourhood, and she has been a great many years about the neighbourhood, and I have seen her many times—I knew her by sight, but not by name—I am quite sure she is the person—I did not know the man she was married to—he signed his name "Highwood," and was married in that name—whether his name was Highwood or not I do not know—I have not got the original book here in which he signed his name—I have a distinct recollection of his telling me that his name was Highwood—I invariably ask the parties their names, and how they spell them.
JOHN TROTT ( police-constable M 193.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 10th of Nov.—I asked at the house where she was residing for Mrs. Highwood—she was brought to the door to me—I told her a person a little way up the street wanted to speak to her—she walked with me to where the first husband was standing—he walked towards her, and as soon as she saw him, she said, "I thought as much; I am living with a man; I am not married to him."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months, without hard labour.
[trialAccount t18421128-258]
258. THOMAS BELGER was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of Nov., 7 panes of glass, value 3 s., the goods of George Wix, and fixed to a building.
GEORGE RUSSEN ( police-constable V 266.)On the 13th of Nov. I was on duty on the Wandsworth-road, and near Vauxhall-gate, I saw the prisoner walking along the road, carrying a sack and something else in his hand—I stopped him, and it was glass, folded in the sack—I asked him where he had brought it from—he said from his master, Mr. Atfield, at Battersea—I saw wet putty on the edge, as if it had been just taken out, and said, "This glass has been just taken out of some place"—he said, "No, it has not, I bought it in a shop in Salmon-street, that is the truth of it"—I took him to the station, and he there said he had taken it out of a glass cupboard where he was living—I went to the house where he said he was living—there was no such a thing as a glass cupboard there, nor had there been—I went to Mr. Atfield, his master, and he had not taken it from there—I went to the shop where he said he had bought it, and they had no recollection of any person buying such a thing there, neither did they know it—I then found Mr. Wix, Prisoner. He has sworn quite false; when he asked me how I came by the glass, I said it belonged to myself; I had it in my hand, not in the sack; I told him I had it from my own home, that I had had it in my possession seven or eight weeks; I had taken it out of a cupboard which I burnt, not being worth the trouble of moving; the sack I borrowed of a labourer; we met another constable; he told me to try and get away, and said, "I will do what I can for you." Witness. I did not.
GEORGE WIX . I am building a house in Battersea-fields—I missed some panes of glass from some sashes there—I cannot identify the glass, but they fit the openings in the sashes, and some pieces of glass which were found on the wall on the premises fit two broken squares found on the prisoner—I noticed the pieces of glass on the wall before the policeman came, but I did not know what they were, and did not disturb them—these are the same number of panes I missed, and they are the same size and quality.
ROBERT COLLINS ( police-constable V 108.) On the 14th of Nov. I went
to Mr. Wix's building in Battersea-fields—I picked up four pieces of glass three in the wash-house, and one about three feet from the window-cill—I matched them with two broken squares taken from the prisoner, and they exactly match.
Prisoner. He did not find them there—I told him I had broken the glass coming along, I heard him tell the other to go and find the pieces, and make it all right, and they winked and nodded to one another.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-259]
259. WILLIAM APPLETON, EDWARD CARRINGTON , and JAMES NICHOLLS were indicted for feloniously assaulting Robert Roger Phillips, on the 8th of Nov., putting him in fear, and taking from his person and against his will, 1 watch, value 30 s., and 1 chain, 5 s., his property; and immediately before, at the time of, and after the said robbery, feloniously beating, striking, and using other personal violence towards him.
ROBERT ROGER PHILLIPS . I am a tailor, and live in Margaret-terrace, Westminster—about one o'clock on the morning of the 8th of Nov. I was returning from my sister-in-law's in William-place, William-street, Pitt-street, Old Kent-road, with my wife, my sister-in-law, and cousin Archibald Penny-cook—I saw a mob at the end of William-street fighting—we had to pass them, and could not do so without touching them—Pennycook was walking a-head of me, and was struck on the hat, I believe by Carrington, but I am not positive—I came up within a foot of him—Appleton asked me what I wanted—I said I wanted to pass—I received a blow from him on the left side of the chest and fell—I rose again, and he struck me, and at the same time caught my watch-guard, which was hanging from my waistcoat-pocket, and ran away with it, watch and all—as I was pursuing him, I believe Carrington gave me a blow or a shove, and I went up against the wall—I did not fall down, but ran again, and was surrounded by the mob—I called "Police!" and "Stop thief!" as bard as I could—the policeman came—I told him I had been robbed of my watch and guard—I could not see Appleton at that time, but I saw Carrington, who was one of the party that shoved me as I was pursuing Appleton, and I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by Mr. CHARNOCK. Q. I suppose it was rather dark? A. There was a gaslight at the corner—I had come from St. Margaret's-terrace to my sister-in-law's between eight and nine o'clock—I had no supper there—I had been drinking nothing to intoxicate me—I had had a drop of ale at my sister's—I cannot exactly say what I had drank—I had no spirits or beer the whole day—I was perfectly sober—I will not swear it was Carrington that struck Pennycook—he shoved against me as I was running from the corner of William's-place to the Kent-road—Carrington was running—there were about twenty people there—I should not say there were fifty.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. What sort of a night was this? A. I think it was rather darkish—the gas was just over our heads where I was struck—I did not get into the midst of the mob—I was standing by the side of them, wanting to pass to go home—I could not go through with out going against some one or other—immediately I came up I said I wanted to pass, and I had no sooner said it than I received a blow that knocked me down—there was no one but Appleton near enough to strike me at that time—I was trying to go through the mob without interfering with any one, but I could not—I know Appleton's features well—he came round the corner afterwards and asked me what was the matter, of his own accord; and I said
"That is the man that stole my watch," and gave him in charge—he came round quite as if he knew nothing of it—he had on, I think, a velvet waistcoat and a red handkerchief or comforter round his neck—he had no hat on—he appeared as if he had been chased—I should say he had run for about 100 yards—when I hallooed "Stop thief!" and "Policeman!" there was a regular bustle all at once—he ran, and I ran ten or a dozen yards after him—I did not lose him till the mob surrounded me.
ARCHIBALD PENNYCOOK . I am a coach-maker, and live in St. Margaret's-terrace, Westminster. I was in the company of the prosecutor and his wife, going along the corner of William-street, about a quarter to one o'clock—there was a mob—when we got up to the people I heard some words, " Go it, Scotty, " or something—I made a halt, because I could not pass without interfering—Appleton came and asked what I wanted—I said I wanted nothing—"Then," said he, "do you want to fight?"—I said, "No"—"What do you want?" said he—I said, "I want nothing but to pass"—he then left me on the left, and I received a blow on my hat, and, turning round, I observed Nicholls with his hand up, and down again on my hat, and so on followed the rest that were round me; as many as could get round me struck my hat—Phillips roared out "Police," and ran off calling "Police"—I leaped out from among them, and ran after him—I stopped at the head of the street—a policeman came up—Phillips told him he had had his watch taken from him—Carrington came and inquired what was the matter, and was given in charge—Appleton then came forward and made the same inquiry, and he was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When you came up there was a fight going on? A. Yes, but I did not see it; I heard the words, " Go it, Scotty "—I had not spoken at all—I was near the centre of the crowd—they got me in among them—the blow Nicholls gave me was not when in the centre of the crowd—it was the second blow I received—I know nothing of his fighting.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. When did you receive the blow? A. As soon as I got to the edge of the mob—my back was towards them—I do not know whether they call it bonneting —it broke my hat, but it did not go over my eyes—Appleton did not strike me—I was not going into the mob—I did not see Appleton do anything.
ISABELLA HOOPER . I was in company with Phillips and Pennycook—my sister was there, but, from ill-usage, is not able to be here to-day—Pennycook received a knock on his hat behind—I said to the person, "What do you knock the young man in that way for? what a shame"—he said, "What a shame!" in a jeering way, and went away—I do not know that person—I only saw one blow struck on his hat at that time—Phillips was just by his side, and was struck by Appleton, as I understood from my sister—his hat was knocked off—I went to pick it up, and some fellow tried to push me down—I took it up, gave it to my sister, and ran after Phillips—I had no bonnet or shawl on at that time, I had left them in the house—I only came out to see them to the corner, just through a little passage into the street—my sister was pushed and knocked about, which has brought on an illness.
JAMES WAUGH ( police-constable M 220.) I was on duty at the Bricklayer's Arms—I ran into the Kent-road, and saw Phillips running and calling "Police"—I went over to him, and asked what was the matter—he said he had lost his watch and chain—while he was talking, Carrington came up and I said to him, "What is this about?"—he said, "I do not know anything at all about it"—I said, "The man says you have assaulted him"—he said he had done nothing—during that time Appleton came up—the prosecutor said, "This is the man that took my watch and chain"—I called the assistance
of another constable, and took them to the station—while there Nicholls came in, and was identified as being one of the parties that had struck Pennycook.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. You had Carrington in custody first? A. Yes—Appleton came up, in a minute or two afterwards, with fourteen or fifteen more, and was picked out—I cannot say that he saw me previous to his coming near me—he came up at the back of the coaches—he had no opportunity of getting away—I called to him, and said, "I shall want you"—he went to the station willingly—when he was charged with the robbery he said he knew nothing at all about it—I bad seen the mob come out of the King's Arms there, and knew they had been there to what they call a Fifth of November supper, which they have had, to my knowledge, for the last ten years—I saw nothing of the fight—I heard of it afterwards—the prosecutor and his party appeared sober, but flurried—they did not appear to have been drinking—Appleton did not appear flurried, as if he had been chased—I searched them, but found nothing on them. NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421128-260]
260. JAMES NICHOLLS and EDWARD CARRINGTON were again indicted for assaulting Archibald Pennycook, with intent to rob him.
ARCHIBALD PENNYCOOK . About one o'clock on the morning of the 8th of Nov., I was with Phillips and Isabella Hooper—there was a mob at the bottom of William-street—I went towards it—Appleton spoke to me, and after he left me I received a blow on the hat—I turned round, and saw Nicholls with his hand held up, which descended on my hat again—I received a blow from a person in the mob which broke the crown of my hat—I did not see Carrington do anything—nothing was done to my person or property to intimate that there was any desire to rob me.
ISABELLA HOOPER . I saw both the prisoners in the mob—I did not see them do anything.
ROBERT ROGER PHILLIPS . I believe that Carrington struck Pennycook, but I am not positive—I saw Nicholls strike him afterwards on his hat—my watch was stolen as soon as I received the blow from Appleton.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421128-261]
261. MARY MACAULIFFE was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of Nov., at Lambeth, 2 coats, value 4 l. 10 s.; 1 hat, 13 s.; 2 pairs of boots, 1 l. 1 s.; 1 table-cloth, 1 s.; 1 tea-pot, 2 s.; 1 kettle, 10 s.; 1 tea-caddy, 4 s.; and 2 candlesticks, 2 s.; the goods of Joseph Kendall, in his dwelling-house; and afterwards, about eight o'clock in the night of the same day, burglariously breaking out of the said dwelling-house
SOPHIA KENDALL . I am the wife of Joseph Kendall, of Agar-cottage, Sidney-place, Clapham-road, in the parish of Lambeth. The prisoner came into our service on the 5th of May, and left on the Monday following—on the 14th of Nov., a little after seven o'clock in the morning, I missed the articles stated, which I had seen safe on Sunday night—when I went down in the morning three doors were open, which had been fastened over night—they had been opened from within—the prisoner was not living with me at this time—I had not seen her on the Sunday—I do not know how she got there—I made the house fast over night, at a little after nine o'clock—I am sure it was after nine—every thing was safe—I cannot say that I particularly observed these articles after nine o'clock—I was in the kitchen, and did not miss anything.
WILLIAM HUMPHRIES ( police-constable M 127.) On the 14th of Nov., about half-past six o'clock in the morning, I met the prisoner coining out of the Alfred's Head at the corner of the London-road, which is about two miles and a half from the prosecutor's—she had a bundle—I stopped her, and
found it contained two coats and two pairs of boots—she said the was going to Whitechapel—I asked what she had there—she said, "Two coats"—I asked where she came from—she said, "From Camberwell"—I asked her how far she had come from the wagon that she came out of—she said, "Only from the Horns"—I asked her if she had been moving—she said, "Yes"—I said it was very early—she said she bad a long way to go, and thought she would have the day before her—she said they were her husband's coats—she was going to have them altered—I asked what alteration they wanted—she said her husband had not left her any thing, and she was going to make what she could of them—I took her to the station—she gave the name of "Mrs. Newnham, Camberwell-grove," as the place she had moved from—I went there, but could not find any such person—I had seen her packing up some things in a washing-tub in the Alfred's Head—I went there, and found a teacaddy, a tea-kettle, a tea-pot, a pair of metal candlesticks, a table-cloth, and a hat tied up in a handkerchief.
ALFRED SPICE ( police-constable V 41.) On the morning of the 14th, about half-past five or twenty minutes to six o'clock, I saw a wagon in the Clapham-road—when it stopped, a basket of apples fell, and the prisoner stepped off the path and picked them up—it was going towards town, in a direction from the prosecutor's house, and about a quarter of a mile from it—I saw a washing-tub on the rods of the wagon, but none of the rest of the property—I have not seen the washing-tub which the officer saw—it is not here—I asked the prisoner whether she was moving—she said from No. 50, Lark-hall-lane—I said there was no such number there—she said there was about half-way up the lane—I asked what the name of the place was—she said, "No particular name, Larkhall-lane."
Prisoner. I hope you will be merciful to me.
GUILTY . Aged 28.
[trialAccount t18421128-262]
262. MARY MACAULIFFE was again indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of Sept., 1 gown, value 1 l.; 6 knives, 1 s.; 6 forks, 1 s.; and 20 paintings and frames, the goods of Mary Ann Pitman, her mistress.
MARY ANN PITMAN . I am a widow, and life at No. 60, Walcot-place, Lambeth. The prisoner was in my service for three weeks—she left on the 6th of Oct.—I missed a quantity of pictures from a closet in the kitchen after she was gone—the policeman brought them afterwards—the closet was opened one day for the carpenter to ease the door, while the prisoner was there—I locked it afterwards—I cannot swear to the knives and forks—I lost some, and these look like mine—this dress is my sister's, and was kept in a wardrobe in the room in which the prisoner slept.
WILLIAM HUMPHRIES ( police-constable M 127.) I took the prisoner into custody on another charge—she was searched at the station, and eight duplicates were found on her, which led to the discovery of this gown at Mr. Wharton's, Mount-street, Lambeth, and some knives and forks, and the pictures at Mr. Dempster's, the pawnbroker's—he is not hero—he gave the property up before the Magistrate—I afterwards went to the different pawnbrokers with the duplicates, and got these articles—I have not got the counterparts of the duplicates here.
ANN STEPHENS . I am the wife of Peter Stephens, a policeman—I am employed at the Southwark-bridge station-house to search females—I searched the prisoner, and found eight duplicates on her, and delivered them to the officer.
( Property produced and sworn to,)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

265. ELLEN MILLGATE was indicted for the wilful murder of a newborn male child.
MARY FRYER . I live with Mr. Pimlott, my brother-in-law, at Camden-place, Camberwell. The prisoner was servant there—on Friday evening, the 21st of October, about half-past eight o'clock, I went up stairs to take her some gruel, and found her lyng on the bed, with some of the clothes on her, and her own clothes on—she had complained of pain in her stomach before that—I told her she had better undress, and get into bed—see did so—I stopped till she was in bed, and saw blood on her clothes—I said, "Ellen, you are unwell," which is a term females use—she said she was—I said, "You appear to be a great deal so"—she said, yes, she was—I then went down into the parlour, to my sister, and told her what I had seen—Mrs. Long the charwoman, came in—I went up afterwards, but know nothing more—I did not see her after eleven o'clock—I saw her again next morning in bed, and took up a band-box, which was on another box in her bed-room—the bandbox felt very heavy—I said, "Ellen, what have you got in this box? stones, or what?"—she said, "I have got gowns and petticoats"—I took off the lid, took out a straw bonnet, and under that thought I saw something like a human body—my sister came into the room, and told Mrs. Long to examine it, and I went from it—I saw her take out the body of a male child—my sister said, "Oh, Ellen, how could you do such a thing as that?"—I did not hear any reply—I did not see her boxes examined, and saw no baby linen found—she was first taken ill about four or five o'clock—she had done her work up to that time—I had observed nothing to induce me to think her in the family way.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had she been long in the service of your brother-in-law? A. She came on the 17th of that month—she continued ill till she left on Saturday—I never attended a person in her situation—my brother-in-law and sister engaged her in consequence of what they observed of her conduct in another service—she appeared a very respectable young woman—she came at their suggestion.
ANN LONG . I am a charwoman. On this Friday evening I went to Mr. Pimlott's house, and went up to the prisoner about ten o'clock in the evening, and found her in bed—I had never seen her before—I asked how she was—she said she was better—I asked how long before she had been unwell—she said two months when she was all right—I observed spots on the floor, and under the carpet, and said I thought it was longer than two months—I have had twelve children myself, and from what I observed I thought there
was a new-born baby—I went down to Mrs. Pimlott—I sat up all night, and went to and fro to her, but was not constantly with her—next morning I assisted in shifting her clothes—Mrs. Fryer took up a band-box, and said, "What have you got? stones, or what?"—I think she said gowns and petticoats—Mrs. Fryer took the lid off, and Mrs. Pimlott said, "Mrs. Long, go and examine that box"—I found a male child dead in it—I thought it had been born a few hours, but it might be twelve—it had not been washed—I saw no marks of violence on it—there was what appeared a dark ribbon round its neck—I cannot say how it was fastened—I did not examine it—Mr. Green, the surgeon, came shortly—the lips were black, and the right side of the face very flat—the after-birth was attached to the child—Mrs. Pimlott said, "Oh, Ellen, how could you ever think to do so?" or something—she made no remark—the child was cold.
THOMAS JOSEPH GREEN . I am a surgeon, and live at Camberwell. I went on Friday evening, at ten o'clock, to Mr. Pimlott's, and saw the prisoner in bed—I asked how she felt—she said, better—I asked the cause of some blood on the floor, which had been pointed out—she said she had been unwell, and it was two or three months since she had been so before—I said it was unusual to lose so much on being unwell suddenly—she said * * * * * * I requested her to show me her breast, which being very dark round the nipple, with the enlargement of some glands, and the appearance of milk on pressure, I told her I felt sure she had been pregnant—nothing more passed that night—I imagined at, that rime that it was an early stage of pregnancy, and that she had miscarried—I gave directions for her treatment, and went away—next morning, at ten o'clock, I was sent for, and went into her room—she was still in bed—I was shown a bundle, which was placed on the floor, and opened—it was a full-grown male—its countenance was distorted—the pupil of the eye dilated, the mouth slightly open—the tongue; protruded, the hands were clenched, and the neck had round it what appeared to be a green ribbon, tied round more than once in a knot—it was tight—the navel string was undivided—the precenta and membranes attached to the other extremity of it—I asked her how long the child had been born before the after-birth followed—she said about five minutes—I examined her person, and found she had been retently delivered of a child—I examined the child on Sunday morning, the 23rd of October—( The witness here entered into a more minute detail of the circumstances, and, upon his cross-examination, stated that he was unable positively to depose that the birth had been complete before life was extinct, and that the ribbon might have been put round the neck in the agony of labour, she being at the time in a state of unconsciousness.
GUILTY of Concealment. — Confined Two Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18421128-267]
267. WILLIAM PURDUE was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of Oct, 23 half-crowns, 22 shillings, and 2 farthings; the monies of Joseph Steel, his master.
JOHN GAMBLE . I am master of the National School at Mortlake. On the 2nd of Nov. I had a little boy in the school named James Benham searched for a whistle—the boy who searched him took a purse from his pocket containing 3 l. 19 s. 6 d. in silver, and two farthings—I kept Benham and the prisoner back after the rest of the boys were discharged—I then asked Benham
where he obtained the money—he said of Purdue, and on questioning Purdue, he said he had taken it from Mr. Steel's till at different times.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Before be told you that, what did you say to him? A. Merely asked him where he had obtained the money—I certainly did not make him any promise or threat, nor was he caned.
COURT. Q. Did he say any thing else? A. Nothing more was said to him till he was examined in the presence of Mr. Steel, and then he again said he had taken it from Mr. Steel's till—I delivered the purse to Mr. Steel, and he gave the same purse back to me next morning—I delivered it into the hands of the officer.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How soon did you take him to Mr. Steel's? A. Perhaps about three hours—he had left school, and I believe went home—his mother lives close by, and I believe is a decent woman—I do not recollect that Mr. Steel told him in my presence that he entirely for-gave him—I will not swear it did take place—to the best of my knowledge it did not—the boy wrote down something on a piece of paper—I do not recollect whether I said I must mention it to the clergyman—it is likely enough—probably, I did say that I must tell "the parson" of it, for fear I should lose my place—I do not remember it—I caused information to be conveyed to the clergyman.
Q. And was it in consequence of that, that this boy was taken up after Mr. Steel had forgiven him? A. I may say yes—I do not recollect whether Mr. Steel said in the presence of several persons that he entirely forgave him—I was these all the time, and I recollect nothing of the kind—it might take place, and I not recollect it, from the agitation arising from the discovery of such a theft—I heard Mr. Steel tell him to go home and make out a statement of what he took and what he spent, and the prisoner brought the statement to me—I have kept the statement—I sent the officer to the minister—I think the prisoner was taken up on the 3rd—both him and Benham were taken at first—the Magistrate took bail for the prisoner's appearance—he has been several years in the school.
JOSEPH STEEL . I am a grocer, and live at Mortlake—the prisoner lived with me as errand-boy—in consequence of receiving information from Mr. Gamble I sent for the prisoner—he came at once—Mr. Gamble questioned him in my presence respecting a purse of money which was found, and before any threat or promise was made the boy said he had taken it from my till—he had lived with me the last time for a month constantly—I knew him before I employed him, and had every reason to be satisfied with him—when he admitted taking the money, I again questioned him, and asked if he had not received it for any bills—he stated he had not, but he took it from the till at various times—I said unless he confessed the whole truth I would send for a policeman, as I thought he had accomplices, and I was willing to forgive him, as I thought him an honest boy—I desired him as well as Mr. Gamble to write it down, but previous to his leaving me I considered I had entirely forgiven him—I intended not to prosecute—I thought him penitent, and intended not to bring him before a court of justice—I thought by pardoning him I should do him more good—I did it from a feeling of humanity more than any thing else—I have no knowledge of this purse, and cannot swear to the money.
Cross-examined. Q. You told him you forgave him? A. Yes, Sir, I believe so—he went away, and was to put down the whole particulars on paper—I certainly never intended to proceed against him—I thought this money was the amount of some bills, as I thought it was impossible be could have taken it out of the till—Mr. Gamble was not there all the time—the paper that the boy wrote out was shown to mo by Mr. Gamble, but I cannot say
when—I was very much annoyed at being sent for by the Magistrate—they told me I was compromising a felony—I told the prisoner I would forgive him in the afternoon when he was brought to me—I really do not remember whether I said I would forgive him before he said he took it—I said I would forgive him, and told him to pray to God to forgive him.
JAMES BENHAM . I am nearly thirteen years old. As I was going to school on Monday, the 31st of October, I saw the prisoner—he gave me some money in a purse—there were some half-crowns and shillings—he asked me to mind it till he went to Mr. Steel's, because it was a bill—my schoolmaster had me searched, and it was found.
Cross-examined. Q. You had been "whistling" in school, I suppose? A. Yes, Sir.
JAMES FINLAYSON ( police-sergtant V 13.) I received information, and waited on Captain Page—he desired me to take the witness Benham and the prisoner into custody—I received this purse and money from Mr. Gamble—here are twenty-three half-crowns, twenty-two shillings, and two farthings.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you wait on anybody else? A. Yes, on Mr. Eades, the minister, and then on Captain Page—I did not know that Mr. Steel had forgiven the prisoner—I heard he would not prosecute him.
JURY to JOSEPH STEEL. Q. Was the till open to the prisoner I A. Yes; he was in the habit of serving and taking pence at times, and he has been in the shop alone.
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.
Confined One Week.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

MARGARET SUSANNAH TRUEFIT . I live in Rotherhithe. Prisoner's Defence. GUILTY .** Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.

273. CHARLES BERRY was indicted for embezzlement.
THOMAS GREEN . I keep the Jacob's-well, Upper ground-street—the prisoner was my pot-boy, and received money for me—on the 9tb of Nov. he asked me to lend him 1 s., which I did—he afterwards came for some beer, and said he was going to take it to the stable for some man—he took it to the stable—I never saw him again till he was in custody—he never paid me any money from Miss James—he ought to have brought 15 s. 4 d.
MATILDA JAMES . I live with my father—on the 9th of Nov. I gave the prisoner a sovereign to pay Mr. Green 15 s. 4 1/2 d.—I did not see him after—I told him to bring me a receipt, and he did not.
WILLIAM ROMAINE ( police-constable L 38.) I took the prisoner—he said he did not know the Jacob's-well, and never lived there.
Prisoner. I never received a farthing of him all the time I was with him—he did not give me sufficient victuals, and I thought it was time to leave.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-274]
274. JAMES GARDINER was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Nov., 3/4 of a pint of gin, value 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Frazer Wilkins, his master, and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS FRAZER WILKINS . I keep the Rose and Crown public-house, Southwark—the prisoner had been in my service three weeks—he slept in a room below me—about six o'clock in the morning of the 1st of Nov. he came down stairs, I was on the watch, and followed him down—he opened one of the doors, and some one gave him in a stone jug—he came to the fountains in the bar, drew some gin in it, and went outside with it—I went to the door, and saw him offering it to a man that stood there—the man said something, and he was bringing the jug back again—I stood in the way, and said, "What are you going to do with that?"—he said, "To draw the watchman a quartern of gin"—I said, "You know you ought not to do that, and you could not be going to do that, because you have got some gin in it, that you have just drawn"—he said he did not know it was wrong, he would not do it again—he said, "I will give him the jug back"—he was going away with the jug—I said, "Stop, you have got some gin in it."
Prisoner. I had opened the house—I said the watchman asked me for some gin which I drew him, and had got the money to pay for it. Witness. He had no right to draw any gin, and he offered me double the money for it.
PATRICK M'CORMACK ( police-constable M 27.) I received charge of the prisoner, and the jug containing gin—the prisoner begged to be allowed to pay for it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not receive the money for the gin laying on the counter? A. Yes.
Prisoner's Defence. I opened the house—the watchman came, and said, "Draw three quartens of gin," which I did, and took it out—the watchman was talking to two or three policemen—as I came back my master asked what I had—I said some gin that I drew for the watchman—the watchman said he did not ask for it—he had put the money in my hand.
JAMES GIFFORD ( police-constable H 169.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18421128-275]
275. WILLIAM SNOWSWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of Nov., 20lbs. of coals, value 3 d.; the goods of Stephen Finney, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
HENRY JOSEPH KING ( Thames police-constable.) On the 29th of Nov. I saw the prisoner row his boat alongside the barge Emma, and get on the gunwale, and throw some pieces of coal in them—he went again, sat on the gunwale a quarter of an hour, and threw some more coals into his boat.
JOHN HOWES . I am a lighterman, of the Emma, in the employ of Joseph Finney—I only knaw his name by what the lighterman say.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421128-276]
276. ROBERT BARFOOT and MARY BARFOOT were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH MOUNT ( police-constable C 115.) At half-past seven in the evening of the 2nd of Nov. I and Russell saw the prisoners and two others in Oxford-street—I watched them down to Charing-cross, and took them in Vine-street—I found no coin on these prisoners—they were remanded till the following Thursday, and then the other two were discharged, and the prisoners kept in custody—I went to a house, No. 122, Cornwall-road, Lambeth—I saw the landlady of the house, Elizabeth Thorn, and her husband—he conducted us to a room in that house—he opened the door—I searched and found under the hob these five counterfeit shillings.
WILLIAM RUSSELL ( police-constable C 113.) I was with Mount, and went to search the room—I found a pipkin, and some metal in it on a cupboard, and some plaster of Paris—there was no one in the room then but the landlord—the prisoners were in custody.
ELIZABETH THORN . I live in No. 121, Cornwall-road—I let the back room first floor of No. 122, to the prisoner, Mary—she lived there eight weeks—she said her husband was a tailor—the officer went into that room.
ROBERT THOMAS . While Mary was lodging at No. 122, Robert used to come there as her husband—I conducted the officers to that room—I saw them find five bad shillings under the hob, and this pipkin and plaster.
Robert Barfoot. Q. When did you see me? A. Repeatedly—on my being brought to Marborough-street, you were sitting down, and I could not positively swear to you, but when you turned round I knew you.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. These 5 shillings are all counterfeit, and all cast in one mould, not finished and have not been circulated—this metal is of a similar kind to that the counterfeit money it made of.
Robert Barfoot's Defence. I was never near, the room—Thorne stated very different at the office to what he has now.
Mary Barfoot's Defence. The lock of the door was not quite sound; any thing might have been put in the room.
ROBERT BARFOOT — GUILTY . Aged 22.
MARY BARFOOT — GUILTY . Aged 23.
Confined One Year.

277. CHARLES MALES was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Dec., 12 pence, and 15 halfpence, the monies of Charles Walter Travers and another, his masters; to which he pleaded
GUILTY. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-278]
278. WILLIAM GARRATT was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Nov., 1 sachrometer and case, value 3 l.; the goods of John Perry.
JOHN PERRY . I am a brewer, and live in St. George's-road, Southwark. This is my sachrometer—I saw it safe on Monday the 21st of Nov., and it was missed on the Monday following—the prisoner had been in my service, but was discharged on the 11th of June last.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you a great many other servants? A. Yes—he was discharged for drunkenness—I had no charge against him for dishonesty.
BENJAMIN HAZLEBY . I am a pawnbroker. I took in this sachrometer of the prisoner, on the 25th of Nov., about six o'clock in the evening.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know him before? A. Not to my knowledge—my masters's shop is in the Lower-road; Deptford—that is rather a populous neighbourhood—it was candle light—there were other customers in the shop, but I do not know that I should know any of them again—I was the only person serving.
COURT. Q. Are you sure he is the man that pawned this? A. Quite sure.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-279]
279. THOMAS HAYES was indicted for a misdemeanor.
SUSAN WINDSOR . I am the wife of William Windsor, of Aldridge-street, Bermondsey New-road. Between five and six o'clock in the evening, of the 1st of December, I went down into the front room found the window open, and the prisoner concealed behind a chair under the window—my sister seized him, and he was given in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. I had been to the hospital to see my sister, and was going home; two men gave me a push, and something I had went over to their house; I knocked at the door, no one came, and I went to the window; I did not know whether the house was empty or not; I went in, then the lodger came home, and they came down and laughed at me.
Witness. He was crouching down concealing himself behind a chair.
GUILTY .* Aged 16. Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18421128-280]
280. JOHN CAMPBELL was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of Nov, 1 coat, value 15 s.; 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; and 1 pair of gloves, 1 s.; the goods of William Wright.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . I am in the employ of Messrs. Cormack, of New-cross, Deptford. On the 11th of Nov., between eleven and twelve o'clock, my coat, handkerchief, and gloves hung in my master's stable—I saw the prisoner in the yard between one and two, with my coat on his arm—I asked what he did there—he said he wanted the kitchen-door—I asked what he wanted there—he said, "To get a bit of bread—I asked what he did with that coat—he said it was not mine—this is the coat—I am sure it is mine—there was no one but him to take it—the door of the harness-room was open—he was walking about the yard—I followed him some distance, he then gave me the coat, and said, "Let me go this once, I will never do it any more," and that he did it for want.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me in the stable? A. No, you were close to my master's house, and close to the harness-room.
JURY. Q. Did you keep your coat hanging up in the harness-room? A. Yes, on a wooden peg—it was possible for other persons to go into the yard.
GUILTY. Aged 27.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, DECEMBER 12 TH, 1842.
